Twin Algebras: Condensable Algebras beyond Anyons
Yuhan Gai, Sakura Schäfer-Nameki, Alison Warman

Abstract
Condensable algebras in 2+1d non-chiral topological orders characterize gapped boundary conditions and interfaces. Applied to the Symmetry Topological Field Theory, they allow classification of symmetric gapped phases and impose sharp constraints on possible phase transitions. A condensable algebra is specified not only by its underlying set of anyons, which end on the boundary or interface, but also by its algebra structure. We introduce the concept of twin condensable algebras, which have the same anyon decomposition, but inequivalent algebra structure. We revisit the classification of condensable algebras in 𝒵 ⁡ ( Vec G ω ) \mathcal{Z}(\text{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}) , i.e. in group-theoretical topological orders for finite groups G G with anomaly ω \omega . In this context we are able to identify twin algebras that arise from different mechanisms, such as subgroup data, SPT cocycles, and symmetry actions. In particular, we construct infinite families of examples of twins from so-called Gassmann triples, and exhibit cases in which the reduced topological orders are inequivalent despite having identical anyon content. Physically, twin algebras describe distinct symmetric phases that have isomorphic spaces of ground states, but inequivalent order parameters. Such twin phases never exhibit relative spontaneous symmetry breaking, and can be used to construct phase transitions without hidden symmetry breaking, which are intrinsically beyond Landau transitions.



I
Introduction

The study of symmetries is vital in the characterization of phases of matter.
Much of the recent excitement has arisen from considering generalized symmetries
Gaiotto:2014kfa
and in particular non-invertible symmetries (for reviews see
Schafer-Nameki:2023jdn
;
Shao:2023gho
;
Bhardwaj:2023kri
). Surprisingly, however, even for finite group symmetries, we can still learn new things. This is the main point of the present paper.

The insights are however a result of developments of systematic tools that honed our ability to study symmetries, gauging, gapped and gapless phases, in the realm of categorical symmetries. In particular in 1+1d, the tools for the study of categorical symmetries have led to an improved understanding and approach to so-called fusion category symmetries, which are the natural generalization of finite group symmetries, and include non-invertible symmetries.

A particularly useful framework is that of the Symmetry topological field theory (SymTFT)
Ji:2019jhk
;
Gaiotto:2020iye
;
Apruzzi:2021nmk
;
Freed:2022qnc
, which allows a systematic exploration of symmetric gapped phases for fusion categories
Bhardwaj:2024qrf
;
Bhardwaj:2024wlr
;
Bhardwaj:2024kvy
;
Chatterjee:2024ych
;
Warman:2024lir
;
Bottini:2025hri
. Here, we will focus mostly on the case of group-theoretical symmetries, i.e. those obtained by gauging of finite group symmetries, however the framework is applicable to any fusion category.

a
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
\mathcal{L}^{\text{sym}}_{\mathcal{S}}
\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}
=
\mathcal{O}_{a}
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{S}}

Figure 1:
The SymTFT for
\mathcal{S}
-symmetric phases:
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{sym}}
is the symmetry boundary (Lagrangian algebra), and
\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}
the physical, and for gapped phases is chosen to be a Lagrangian algebra. The interval compatification gives rise to the phase
\mathcal{P}
. Local order parameters re
\mathcal{O}_{a}
, that arise from anyons in the SymTFT (generalized charges) that end on both boundaries.

Gapped boundary conditions.

The SymTFT takes in a symmetry
\mathcal{S}
and realizes it on the gapped boundary of a 2+1d TQFT. Putting this theory on a finite interval, with a gapped second boundary, the physical boundary, gives rise to a gapped phase, see
Figure

1
. Thus, clearly, studying gapped boundary conditions of the SymTFT is paramount to understanding gapped phases in 1+1d. Examples we will focus on are
\mathcal{S}
that are obtained from finite group symmetries
G
, possibly after gauging. The SymTFT is the
G
-gauge theory or Dijkgraaf-Witten theory (with a twist, if
G
has an ’t Hooft anomaly
\omega
). The topological line defects of the SymTFT form a braided fusion category, the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
, and will be referred to as anyons.
The SymTFT paradigm now states, that gapped boundary conditions are in 1-1 correspondence with so-called Lagrangian algebras in the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
of
\mathcal{S}
.
Lagrangian algebras are decomposable as non-negative integer combination of anyons
a_{i}

\mathcal{L}=\bigoplus_{i}n_{i}a_{i}\,.

(I.1)

However crucially, there is an algebra structure, i.e multiplication

m:\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{L}\,.

(I.2)

The multiplication
m
is an element in the vector space
\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})}(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L})
.
It is this algebra structure that we will be most interested in in this paper.

Similarly condensable algebras more generally are étale algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
, which need not be of maximal dimension. See
Section

A.1
for definition of condensable and Lagrangian algebras. Lagrangian algebras are condensable algebras of maximal dimension. Non-Lagrangian condensable algebras organize gapless phases
Chatterjee:2022tyg
;
Bhardwaj:2023bbf
;
Wen:2023otf
;
Bhardwaj:2024qrf
.

Twin Algebras.

We define twin algebras to be condensable algebras, which have the same anyon decomposition
\oplus_{i}n_{i}a_{i}
but a different multiplication
m
.
Lagrangian algebras with this property are referred to as twin Lagrangian algebras.
Twin algebras give rise to extremely interesting phases and phase transitions, which will be explored, both here and in the companion paper
WGS
.

However,
in general it is hard to find or even classify condensable algebras in modular tensor categories. Even for group-theoretical fusion categories, where the SymTFT has defects in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
, we found that there are some loopholes in the literature which we will close before embarking on a general study of twin algebras.

Group-theoretical Symmetries.

We focus on group-theoretical symmetries
which can be obtained by gauging (Morita dual
1

1

Section

for the definition of Morita dual and the notation
\mathcal{C}(G,\omega,K,\beta):=(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})_{\mathcal{M}(K,\beta)}^{*}
.
) from group symmetries
G
with anomaly
\omega\in H^{3}(G,U(1))
, i.e.
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
. Physically, these are symmetries obtained from

by stacking a
K

and gauging a subgroup

G
.

As the Drinfeld center is invariant under gauging the symmetry, luckily all these categories have the same SymTFT:

\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}(G,\omega,K,\beta))\cong\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})\,.

(I.3)

To study symmetires and phases, we will be interested in the condensable algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
. These are classified and labeled by

A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)\,,

(I.4)

where
N\triangleleft H\subset G
are subgroups,
\gamma

\omega
on
N
, and together with
\epsilon
, they define
\alpha\in H^{3}(H/N,U(1))
that pulls back to
\omega
on
H

Davydov2009ModularIF
;
davydov2017lagrangian
;
Hannah:2023tae
.
These give rise to interfaces from
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
to the reduced TO

\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha})

(I.5)

where
\alpha
depends on
\omega
and the SPT data of the algebra and is defined in (
A.2
).
Very broadly speaking, in terms of
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
-symmetric phases, the ones corresponding to
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
have preserved symmetry
H
,
N
labels the support of the twisted sectors,
\gamma
modifies the multiplication of (twisted) operators and
\epsilon
modifies the symmetry action on twisted operators. The Lagrangian algebras are singled out by
H=N
and
\epsilon
is fixed in terms of
\gamma
. We will denote Lagrangian algebras by

\mathcal{L}(H,\gamma):=A(H,H,\gamma,\epsilon(\gamma))\,.

(I.6)

These are the gapped boundary conditions, obtained by stacking
\gamma
and gauging
H
on the Dirichlet boundary that realizes
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
, i.e.
\mathcal{L}(1,1)
.

However, this classification has redundancies. We identify the isomorphic one in
Section

II.1.2
. This is important for physical applications because, we are interested in distinct, inequivalent symmetric gapped phases.

Twins for Group-theoretical Symmetries.

With this precise (and free of redundancies) classification at hand, we can then explore the landscape of algebras systematically. In particular we are able to identify criteria for when algebras are twin in terms of the classification data (
I.4
).

We find twin algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
of different types, which can be distinguished by the data in (
I.4
) and will be referred to as
H
-,
N
-,
\gamma
- and
\epsilon
-type twin algebras, which differ in these entries of the algebra classification datum. Their anyon decomposition is the same, but the algebra structure differs.

Twin algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
are closely related to
Gassmann triples

Sunada1985RiemannianCA
;
Gassmann:1926
, which we review in
Section

III.1.2
: let
H_{i}
be subgroups of
G
.

(G,H_{1},H_{2})
form a Gassmann triple if

|H_{1}\cap[g]|=|H_{2}\cap[g]|

(I.7)

for any conjugacy class
[g]
of
G
. Note that these can also be trivial if
H_{1}=gH_{2}g^{-1}
for some
g\in G
.
For twins in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
, we show that their
H
and
N
subgroups have to form Gassmann triples of
G
.

Twin algebras can be distinguished by the different algebra structure on the same set of anyons, however, we have found other interesting imprints of them. E.g. found non-maximal (i.e. non-Lagrangian) twin algebras of
H
-type can lead to inequivalent reduced TOs (
I.5
) and give examples of these in
Section

III.1.3
.

The
\gamma
-type twin algebras include the ones obtained from Bogomolov multiplier
Pollmann:2012
;
Davydov:2013xov
;
Kobayashi:2025ykb
;
Kobayashi:2025pxs
. We also found
\gamma
-type twins beyond Bogomolov multipliers.
The
\epsilon
-type twin algebras define twin phases that can be distinguished using generalized string order parameters.

Physical Implications.

The physical content of twin algebras is developed in
Section

IV
and the compatnion paper
WGS
. Twin Lagrangian algebras
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
in the
SymTFT
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
define
twin gapped phases

\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}},\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}
via
interval compactification with a fixed symmetry boundary. Although these
phases share the same generalized-charges, they are physically
inequivalent: their algebras of order parameters differ. We make this
precise by recovering the (twisted) local-operator algebra of a phase

\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}}
directly from the
condensable algebra
\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}
via the forgetful functor

F_{{\mathcal{S}}}:\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathcal{S}\,,

(I.8)

with multiplication
inherited from the comultiplication on
\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}
. Twin algebras,
by definition, agree as objects and disagree precisely on this
multiplication. The main consequence is a sharp dichotomy between twin and non-twin Lagrangians:
two phases exhibit
no relative
spontaneous symmetry breaking (SSB)
:
for every fixed choice of symmetry boundary (i.e. for all symmetries related by gauging, or Morita equivalent symmetries), the twin phases do not have relative SSBing, i.e. they have the same number of vacua (of course the number of vacua depends on the choice of symmetry). Twin gapped phases are therefore the natural candidates for direct transitions
without hidden symmetry breaking, and, equipped with an anomaly, for intrinsically beyond Landau transitions.
concrete realizations are the
subject of
Section

VI
and the companion paper
WGS
. These provide new examples of DQCPs
Senthil:2023vqd
in 1+1d, which remain DQCPs for any Morita equivalent symmetry.

II
Condensable Algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})

Let
G
be any finite group and
\omega\in H^{3}(G,U(1))
.
A condensable algebra in the Drinfeld center
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
is classified in
davydov2017lagrangian
;
Hannah:2023tae
, which, however, contains some redundancies, which we will identify in
Section

II.1.2
.
Physically, such equivalence classes characterize all possible inequivalent interfaces from
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
topological order (TO) to a reduced TO.
For physical applications, where the topological defects in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
play the role of generalized charges, it is useful to have a decomposition of the algebras in terms of anyons.
In
Section

II.2
we provide a derivation of the anyon decomposition for any condensable algebra in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
. However, inequivalent algebras can have the same anyon decomposition, meaning they are isomorphic as objects. Such pairs of algebras will be called twin algebras, and are defined in
Section

II.2
. Finally we discuss the partial order (Hasse diagram) on condensable algebras in
Section

II.5
and the interfaces and reduced TOs in
Section

II.4
.

II.1
Classification of Condensable Algebras

The condensable algebras and their reduced TOs (whose definitions are recalled in
Appendix

A
)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
were previously discussed in
Davydov2009ModularIF
;
davydov2017lagrangian
;
Hannah:2023tae
.
Here, we review this classification in
Section

II.1.1
. We then determine the isomorphism classes of condensable algebras in Lemma
II.5
(with proof provided in Appendix
B.1
).

II.1.1
Condensable Algebras

The following description of condensable algebras in the TO
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
and their corresponding reduced TO were proven in
davydov2017lagrangian
:

Theorem II.1

(
davydov2017lagrangian
, Thm 3.15)
.

Let
G
be a finite group and
\omega\in H^{3}(G,U(1))
.
A condensable algebra in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
is determined by:

•

H\subseteq G
is a subgroup;

•

N\triangleleft H
is a normal subgroup;

•

\gamma:N\times N\rightarrow U(1)
such that
d\gamma=\omega|_{N\times N\times N}
;

•

\epsilon:H\times N\rightarrow U(1)
satisfying compatibility conditions (see
Appendix

A
),

denote the corresponding algebra by
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
.

We refer to
\gamma
as the
SPT
and
\epsilon
the
H
-action phase
. These algebras give rise to interfaces between
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
and a reduced TO which is determined as follows:

Theorem II.2

(
davydov2017lagrangian
, Thm 3.16)
.

The reduced TO corresponding to
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
is

\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})_{A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)}^{\text{loc}}\cong\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha}),

(II.1)

where
\alpha\in H^{3}(H/N,U(1))
is determined by
\omega,\gamma,\epsilon
, with an explicit expression given by formula (
A.2
).

A special case are Lagrangian algebras, which are condensable algebras of maximal dimension (see
Appendix

A
for definition)
davydov2013witt
, which give rise to gapped boundary conditions:

Corollary II.3

(Lagrangian algebras)
.

A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
is a Lagrangian algebra if and only if
H=N
. In this case
\epsilon
is uniquely determined by
\gamma
(see App
A
), hence denote a Lagrangian algebra by
\mathcal{L}(H,\gamma)
.

An example of Lagrangian algebras are

A(1,1)
, which realizes the
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}
symmetry, also known as the Dirichlet boundary.
For trivial
\omega
, there is also the boundary
\mathcal{L}(G,1)
obtained by gauging
G
, which defines the associated Neumann
\mathsf{Rep}(G)
symmetry boundary.
More generally
\mathcal{L}(H,\gamma)
is the gapped boundary condition, obtained from the
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}
Dirichlet boundary after stacking with
\gamma
and gauging
H
.

The
algebra structure
of
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
, whose objects can be modeled as
G
-graded vector spaces equipped with
G
-actions, is made explicit in
Davydov2009ModularIF
;
Hannah:2023tae
. We summarize the algebra structure as follows:
As a vector space over
{\mathbb{C}}
,
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
is spanned by
v_{g,n}
,
g\in G,n\in N
,
subject to the relation

v_{gh,n}=\frac{\epsilon(h,n)}{\omega(g,h|n)}v_{g,hnh^{-1}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall h\in H,

(II.2)

where
\omega(g,h|n)\in U(1)
is the transgression of
\omega
, whose explicit formula can be found in (
A.10
).
Fixing a set
R
of representatives for
G/H
fixes a basis

B=\{v_{r,n}\,|\,r\in R,n\in N\}\,.

(II.3)

which implies that the dimension of the condensable algebra
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
is

\text{dim}(A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon))=\frac{|G|\times|N|}{|H|}\,.

(II.4)

The
G
-grading is

|v_{g,n}|=gng^{-1},

(II.5)

and the
G
-action is

g^{\prime}\cdot v_{g,n}=\omega(g^{\prime},g|n)\,v_{g^{\prime}g,n},

(II.6)

where
g,g^{\prime}\in G
and
n,n^{\prime}\in N
. Multiplication is defined on the spanning set by

v_{g,n}v_{g^{\prime},n^{\prime}}=\delta_{gH,g^{\prime}H}\frac{\gamma(n,n^{\prime})}{\Omega_{g}(n,n^{\prime})}v_{g,nn^{\prime}}\,,

(II.7)

here,
\Omega_{g}:N\times N\rightarrow U(1)
trivializes
2
2

2

That is,
d\Omega_{g}=\omega/\omega^{g}
. We introduce notations:
{}^{g}x:=gxg^{-1}
and
f^{g}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}):=f({}^{g}x_{1},\cdots,{}^{g}x_{n})
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, group elements

,

and function

.

\omega/\omega^{g}
, whose explicit formula in terms of

).

The dimension of

is
|G||N|/|H|
.
For a fixed

\{v_{r,1}\,|\,r\in R\}
forms a complete system of orthogonal idempotents in
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
.

However, this description contains redundancies. In applications, identifying non-isomorphic algebras is key, e.g., when classifying distinct phases of matter.
Some of the redundancies were pointed out in
davydov2017lagrangian
as follows:

Lemma II.4

(
davydov2017lagrangian
, Lemma 3.12)
.

Given two condensable algebras
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
and
A(H,N,\gamma^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
, if there exists
c:N\rightarrow U(1)
such that

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}(n_{1},n_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{c(n_{1}n_{2})}{c(n_{1})c(n_{2})}\gamma(n_{1},n_{2}),

(II.8)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}(h,n)

\displaystyle=\epsilon(h,n)\frac{c({}^{h}n)}{c(n)},

(II.9)

for any
n_{1},n_{2},n\in N
,
h\in H
and
{}^{h}n:=hnh^{-1}
,
then
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
and
A(H,N,\gamma^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})
are isomorphic algebras.

From now on, we always take such identification into account. The possible
\gamma
’s in
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
form an
H^{2}(N,U(1))
-torsor. The above still does not identify all the isomorphic algebras, which we address next.

II.1.2
Isomorphism Classes of Condensable Algebras

We now identify the complete set of isomorphism classes of condensable algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
, with proof given in
Section

B.1
. This will then allow us to identify distinct phases in applications to quantum systems.

Lemma II.5

(Isomorphisms of Condensable Algebras)
.

Two condensable algebras
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
and
A(H^{\prime},N^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
are isomorphic, if and only if there exists
g\in G
such that

\displaystyle H^{\prime}

\displaystyle={}^{g}\!H,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}({}^{g}n_{1},{}^{g}n_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma(n_{1},n_{2})}{\Omega_{g}(n_{1},n_{2})},

(II.10)

\displaystyle N^{\prime}

\displaystyle={}^{g}\!N,

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}({}^{g}h,{}^{g}n)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Omega_{g}({}^{h}n,h)}{\Omega_{g}(h,n)}\epsilon(h,n),

where we use notation:
{}^{g}x=gxg^{-1}
,
{}^{g}\!H=gHg^{-1}
, for
h\in H
and
n_{1},n_{2},n\in N
and
\Omega_{g}
is defined as

\Omega_{f}(g,h)=\frac{\omega(f,g,h)\omega({}^{f}\!g,{}^{f}h,f)}{\omega({}^{f}\!g,f,h)},

(II.11)

Proof.

See Appendix
B.1
.
∎

The relation for Lagrangian algebras is then simply:

Corollary II.6

(Isomorphism of Lagrangian Algebras)
.

Lagrangian algebras
\mathcal{L}(H,\gamma)
and
\mathcal{L}(H^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
are isomorphic if and only if
H^{\prime}={}^{g}\!H
and
\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma^{g^{-1}}\Omega_{g^{-1}}
for some
g\in G
.

This agrees with the classification of indecomposable module categories over group-theoretical fusion categories

(also see
Ostrikmodule

II.2
Anyon Decomposition of Algebras

For applications, e.g. to the classification of symmetric phases, it is informative to have a decomposition of the algebras in terms of anyons. For instance, they correspond to the generalized charges.
Here, we provide a formula to decompose each condensable algebra
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
into simple anyons. This is achieved using characters of objects in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
, studied in
Bantay:1993ku
;
davydov2017lagrangian
.

Recall that objects in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
are
G
-graded vector spaces with
\omega
-projective
G
-actions
davydov2017lagrangian
. A simple object in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
is labeled by
([x],\rho)
, where
[x]\subset G
is a conjugacy class
and
\rho
an irreducible (normalized)
(\omega(\cdot,\cdot|x)^{-1})
-projective representation of the centralizer
C_{G}(x)
(of a representative
3
3

3

For each
x\in[x]
, denote the representation of
C_{G}(x)
by
\rho_{x}
. For each
f\in[x]
, the characters of
\rho_{f}
are related to those of
\rho_{x}
by means of
(
gruen2021computing
, equation (2.10))
.

x\in[x]
).

Proposition II.7

(Anyon Decomposition)
.

Condensable algebra
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
has decomposition:

A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)\cong\bigoplus_{([x],\rho)}n_{([x],\rho)}([x],\rho),

(II.12)

with multiplicity of simple object
([x],\rho)
being

n_{([x],\rho)}=\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in[x],\\
g\in C_{G}(f)\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\biggl\{\frac{\overline{\Tr(\rho_{f}(g))}}{|G|}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in R,\\
{}^{\bar{y}}f\in N,g^{y}\in H\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{\omega({}^{\bar{y}}g,y^{-1}|f)}{\omega(y^{-1},g|f)}\epsilon({}^{\bar{y}}g,{}^{\bar{y}}f)\biggr\},

(II.13)

with
\overline{\Tr(\rho_{f}(g))}
the complex conjugation of
\Tr(\rho_{f}(g))
.

Proof.

Recall from
davydov2017lagrangian
that for any object
V
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
, its character is defined on commuting pairs of elements
f,g\in G
,
fg=gf
, to be

\chi_{V}(f,g)=\Tr_{V_{f}}(g)\,,

(II.14)

where
V_{f}
denotes the
f
-graded component of
V
, and it takes the value of zero on non-commuting elements.
The scalar product of characters
\chi_{V},\chi_{W}
of objects
V,W
of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
is defined by

\langle\chi_{V},\chi_{W}\rangle:=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f,g\in G\\
fg=gf\end{subarray}}\overline{\chi_{V}(f,g)}\;\chi_{W}(f,g)

(II.15)

and satisfies the orthogonality relation

\langle\chi_{V},\chi_{W}\rangle=\text{dim}\left(\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})}(V,W)\right)\,.

(II.16)

Applying
(
davydov2017lagrangian
, Lemma 5.6)
, we can compute the character of the condensable algebra
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
to be

\chi_{A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)}(f,g)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in R,\\
{}^{\bar{y}}f\in N,\,{}^{\bar{y}}g\in H\end{subarray}}\frac{\omega({}^{\bar{y}}g,y^{-1}|f)}{\omega(y^{-1},g|f)}\epsilon({}^{\bar{y}}g,{}^{\bar{y}}f)\,,

(II.17)

for
f,g\in G
such that
fg=gf
, and
R
any complete set of cosets of
G/H
. The character vanishes on pairs of non-commuting group elements. The orthogonality relation (
II.16
) leads us to expression (
II.13
) as claimed.
∎

Example II.8

(Abelian Groups)
.

To illustrate the above formula (
II.13
), we use it to decompose any condensable algebra
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{A})
, where
A
is a finite abelian group, as follows:

A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in N,\,\widehat{a}\in\widehat{A},\\
\widehat{a}|_{H}=\epsilon(\cdot,a)\end{subarray}}(a,\widehat{a})\,.

(II.18)

This follows by starting with the general expression

A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)=\bigoplus_{(a,\widehat{a})\in A\times\widehat{A}}n_{a,\widehat{a}}(a,\widehat{a}),

(II.19)

and applying equation (
II.13
), which simplifies to

n_{a,\widehat{a}}=\begin{cases}0,&a\notin N,\\
\langle\widehat{a},\epsilon(\cdot,a)\rangle_{H},&a\in N,\end{cases}

(II.20)

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H}
denotes the scalar product of (class) functions
4
4

4

More explicitly, let
\chi,\psi:H\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
be two (class) functions, define scalar product
\langle\chi,\psi\rangle_{H}:=1/|H|\sum_{h\in H}\chi(h^{-1})\psi(h)
.
on
H
. Hence equation (
II.18
) holds.

Example II.9

(Lagrangian algebras)
.

Note that for a Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}(H,1)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
, the multiplicity
n_{([1],\rho)}
of anyon
([1],\rho)
, where
\rho
denotes an irreducible representation of
G
, agrees with the multiplicity of the trivial representation of
H
in
\text{Res}^{G}_{H}\rho
. This follows from:

\begin{split}n_{([1],\rho)}&=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\overline{\Tr(\rho(g))}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in R\\
{}^{\bar{y}}g\in H\end{subarray}}1\\
&=\langle\chi_{\rho},\text{Ind}^{G}_{H}1\rangle_{G}\\
&=\langle\text{Res}^{G}_{H}\chi_{\rho},1\rangle_{H},\end{split}

(II.21)

where
\chi_{\rho}
denotes the character of
\rho
and the final line follows from Frobenius reciprocity.

For more specific examples,

\mathcal{L}(1,1)\cong\!\!\bigoplus_{r_{i}\in\mathsf{Rep}(G)}\text{dim}(r_{i})([1],r_{i})

(II.22)

in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})

defines the Lagrangian algebra for the Dirichlet boundary for
\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
symmetry, and

\mathcal{L}(G,1)\cong\bigoplus_{[g]}([g],1)

(II.23)

in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
defines the Neumann symmetry boundary for
\mathsf{Rep}(G)
symmetry.

II.3
Twin Algebras

It is rather common to refer to algebras simply in terms of their anyon decomposition. However, there can be algebras whose anyon decomposition is the same, but whose multiplicative structure is different. I.e.,
there can be non-isomorphic condensable algebras but isomorphic as objects:

Definition II.10
.

Two condensable algebras
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
in a modular tensor category
\mathcal{B}
are called
twin algebras
(or simply
twins
) if
A_{1}\cong A_{2}
as objects but
A_{1}\not\cong A_{2}
as algebras in
\mathcal{B}
.

Twin algebras exist in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
, both Lagrangian and non-maximal condensable algebras.
We provide a characterization of twins in terms of the classification data
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
, in
Section

III
.

II.4
Interfaces from Condensable Algebras

One of the main applications of non-maximal condensable algebras is that they define topological interfaces between topological orders.
Here we will describe interfaces
\mathcal{I}_{A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)}
associated to
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
, which define interfaces from

\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
to the reduced topological order, which is given by
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha})
by Theorem
II.2
:

\mathcal{I}_{A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)}
\mathcal{Z}(G,{\omega})
\mathcal{Z}({H/N},{\alpha})

(II.24)

A description in terms of anyons was given in many instances in
Beigi:2010htr
;
Chatterjee:2022tyg
;
Bhardwaj:2023bbf
;
Bhardwaj:2024qrf
;
Bhardwaj:2025jtf
.
By “folding” along the interface
\mathcal{I}_{A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)}
, we can associate to the condensable algebra
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)

a Lagrangian algebra in the folded setup

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\text{folded}}(H^{\text{diag}},\varphi)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})\boxtimes\overline{\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha})}

(II.25)

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G\times H/N}^{\omega\times\overline{\alpha}})\,,

where

•

denoting by
p:H\rightarrow H/N
the projection,

H^{\text{diag}}=\{(h,p(h))\;:\;h\in H\}\,.

(II.26)

•

\varphi
is a 2-cocycle on
H^{\text{diag}}
such that
A^{\text{folded}}(H^{\text{diag}},\varphi)
contains

\displaystyle A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)\boxtimes A(H/N,1,1,1)

(II.27)

\displaystyle=A(H\times H/N,N\times 1,\widetilde{\gamma},\widetilde{\epsilon}),

as a subalgebra. Here,
A(H/N,1,1,1)
is the trivial condensable algebra in
\overline{\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha})}

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}((n_{1},1),(n_{2},1))

\displaystyle=\gamma(n_{1},n_{2})\,,

(II.28)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\epsilon}((h_{1},p(h_{2})),(n,1))

\displaystyle=\epsilon(h_{1},n)\,,

for
n_{1},n_{2},n\in N
,
h_{1},h_{2}\in H
.

Note that
\omega\times\overline{\alpha}
trivializes on
H^{\text{diag}}
because
p^{*}\alpha
is equivalent to
\omega|_{H}

Hannah:2023tae
. Hence
A^{\text{folded}}(H^{\text{diag}},\varphi)
exists.

Lagrangian algebras in the folded theory corresponding to a condensable algebra are not unique. They can be related via an autoequivalence on the reduced TO. For example, consider the subgroup

H^{2}(H/N,U(1))\subset\text{Aut}^{\text{br}}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha})),

(II.29)

an element
\phi
in this subgroup
H^{2}(H/N,U(1))
induces a 2-cocycle
p^{*}\phi
on
H^{\text{diag}}
via pullback along the projection
p:H^{\text{diag}}\rightarrow H/N
.
p^{*}\phi
defines an autoequivalence that relates the two Lagrangian algebras

\mathcal{L}(H^{\text{diag}},\varphi),\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{L}(H^{\text{diag}},\varphi(p^{*}\phi)),

(II.30)

in the folded theory
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G\times H/N}^{\omega\times\overline{\alpha}})
.

Map of Anyons.
As mentioned, a condensable algebra defines a map on anyons from the reduced TO to the original TO, which can be read off from the folded Lagrangian. The folded Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}^{\text{folded}}(H^{\text{diag}},\varphi)
can be decomposed in terms of the anyons using (
II.13
)

\mathcal{L}^{\text{folded}}(H^{\text{diag}},\varphi)=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})\\
b\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha})\end{subarray}}n_{ab}\;{a}\otimes\overline{b}\,.

(II.31)

The anyon decomposition of
A^{\text{folded}}(H^{\text{diag}},\varphi)
encodes a map of anyons from the reduced TO
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha})
to the original TO
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\mapsto a\,.

(II.32)

The anyons in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
that do not appear in the image of this map are confined and do not pass through the interface: for example, the anyons
([x],\rho)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
such that
[x]\cap H=\emptyset
are confined.
Note that as an equivalence of modular tensor categories, such a map preserves spins and braidings, i.e. it needs to be compatible with modular data as discussed in
Chatterjee:2022tyg
;
Ng:2023wsc
, in particular:

\displaystyle Sn=nS^{\prime}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptTn=nT^{\prime}\,,

(II.33)

where
S,T
and
S^{\prime},T^{\prime}
denote the modular matrices of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
and
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha})
respectively and
n
is the matrix of non-negative integer coefficients appearing in (
II.31
).

Note that the compatibility conditions on objects and modular data do not capture the full algebra structure of condensable algebras, and the modular data does not fully specify an MTC
mignard2021modular
;
Delaney:2021owx
, which is why we impose the stronger requirement of algebra inclusion of (
II.27
) in
A^{\text{folded}}(H^{\text{diag}},\varphi)
.

II.5
Partial Order and Hasse Diagram

Condensable algebras for a fixed topological order have a partial order on them. This has surprising connections to the structure of phases and phase-transitions, and provides a useful way to characterize all symmetric phases for a given symmetry
Bhardwaj:2024qrf
. In particular given that it is independent of the symmetry boundary and only depends on the bulk topological order, it is the same Hasse diagram for all Morita equivalent symmetries.

We now define the partial order on condensable algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
and the resulting Hasse diagram:

Proposition II.11
.

There is a partial order on condensable algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
defined by:

A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)\preceq A(H^{\prime},N^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime})

(II.34)

if

H=H^{\prime}\,,\hskip 10.00002ptN<N^{\prime}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}|_{N}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\epsilon=\epsilon^{\prime}|_{H\times N}

(II.35)

or

H^{\prime}<H\,,\hskip 10.00002ptN=N^{\prime}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}\,,\hskip 10.00002pt\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon|_{H^{\prime}\times N^{\prime}}\,.

(II.36)

Proof.

See Appendix
B.2
.
∎

A useful depiction of the partial order is the Hasse diagram of condensable algebras
Bhardwaj:2024qrf
, which uses the partial order and arranges the algebras by quantum dimension
where

\text{dim}(A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon))={|G||N|\over|H|}\,.

(II.37)

The Lagrangian algebras, as the category, have quantum dimension
|G|
. The Hasse diagram is obtained by arranging algebras by quantum dim along the vertical axis, starting at the top with
A(G,1,1,1)=1
and the Lagrangian algebras at the bottom, and two algebras are connected, if they satisfy
A\preceq A^{\prime}
.

There are interesting features of the Hasse diagram, which have deep implications in the structure of phases and transitions.

An example considers the application to direct phase transitions:
Define
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
as a
sub-Lagrangian algebra
, if it is non-Lagrangian,
\text{dim}(A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon))<|G|
, but there are no non-Lagrangian algebras that it is contained in. Then:

Corollary II.12
.

Any sub-Lagrangian algebra is contained in one or two Lagrangian algebras. If it is only contained in one Lagrangian, the reduced topological order is anomalous.

Proof.

Fix any non-Lagrangian algebra
A=A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
. As a consequence of Proposition
II.11
, it is always a subalgebra of the Lagrangian algebra of the form
\mathcal{L}(N,\gamma)
.
If
A
is also a subalgebra of another Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}(H^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
for some subgroup
H^{\prime}\subset G
such that
N\subsetneq H^{\prime}
. We have
\gamma^{\prime}|_{N}=\gamma
. Then by Proposition
II.11
,
A
is also a subalgebra of
A(H^{\prime},N,\gamma,\epsilon^{\prime}|_{H^{\prime}\times N})
, where
\epsilon^{\prime}
is fully determined by
\gamma^{\prime}
via (
A.9
).
By assumption
A
is a sub-Lagrangian algebra, and thus is not contained in any other non-Lagrangian algebra, either:

•

\mathcal{L}(H^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
does not exist, in which case
A
is a subalgebra of a single Lagrangian algebra. In this case,
\omega
is not trivializable on
H
(otherwise
A(H^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
exists for
H^{\prime}=H
) and the anomaly
\alpha\in H^{3}(H/N,U(1))
(expressed in terms of
\omega,\gamma,\epsilon
in (
A.2
)) appearing in the reduced TO
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha})
is non-trivial. Or:

•

If
\mathcal{L}(H^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
exists, then
H^{\prime}=H
,
A
is a subalgebra of two Lagrangian algebras:
\mathcal{L}(N,\gamma)
and
\mathcal{L}(H^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
.

∎

This means the bottom of the Hasse diagram – comprised of the sub-Lagrangian and Lagrangian algebras – has only two types of connections:

\boxed{A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)}
\boxed{\mathcal{L}(N,\gamma)}
\boxed{\mathcal{L}(H,\gamma^{\prime})}
\boxed{A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)}
\boxed{\mathcal{L}(N,\gamma)}

(II.38)

If a sub-Lagrangian algebra is contained in two Lagrangians, it has an interpretation in terms of a direct phase transition between the gapped phases, that these two Lagrangian algebras represent.

If the sub-Lagrangian algebra is contained in only one Lagrangian algebra then its reduced TO
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha})
has non-trivial anomaly
\alpha
(
A.2
) and in fact it is possible to interpret this (with a particular choice of symmetry) as an igSPT
Wen:2023otf
;
Bhardwaj:2024qrf
. It only admits two condensable algebras: the trivial algebra and the Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}(1,1)
. Equivalently,
\mathsf{Vec}_{H/N}^{\alpha}
only admits a single topological SSB phase. Hence if the sub-Lagrangian algebra of interest results in a gapless SPT with respect to some symmetry, then it is intrinsically gapless.

III
Twin Algebras

We discussed the comprehensive classification (and crucially, the identification up to isomorphism) of condensable algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
. Also we defined the concept of twin algebras in
Section

II.3
. These algebras are thus far only sporadically studied.
Given the classification of algebras in terms of
(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
, we can divide them as follows – most of these have to our knowledge not been discussed in the literature before:

1.

Twins with non-conjugate subgroups
H
.
Any pair of condensable algebras with the same anyon decomposition but whose subgroups are not conjugate, define a pair of twin algebras. One family consists of algebras with non-conjugate subgroups
H\subset G
, constructed from so-called Gassmann triples, which we study in
Section

III.1
. These new twin algebras can be either maximal or non-maximal (non-Lagrangian). We call these
twins of
H
-type
.

2.

Twins with non-conjugate subgroups
N_{i}
.

In turn we could have the same group
H
, but non-conjugate normal subgroups. Again, such examples have not been discussed to our knowledge. For such twins, the subgroups
N_{i}
necessarily also form Gassmann triple.
We call these
twins of
N
-type
.

3.

Twins with inequivalent SPTs
\gamma
.

Fixing the same
H,N
but allowing
\gamma
to differ, there can be two types of twin algebras: First type contains Lagrangian algebras, which have been previously considered, whose 2-cocycles
\gamma
differ by a Bogomolov multiplier
Pollmann:2012
;
Davydov:2013xov
;
Cong:2017ffh
;
Kobayashi:2025ykb
;
Kobayashi:2025pxs
. We review this in
Section

III.3
.
Secondly, we find new, non-maximal condensable algebras for anomalous symmetries whose 2-cocycles do not differ by a Bogomolov multiplier. We call these
twins of
\gamma
-type
.

4.

Twins with inequivalent
H
-action phase
\epsilon
.

Finally, in
Section

III.4
, we construct twin algebras that differ by
\epsilon
but have the same
H,N,\gamma
. These new twin algebras are non-maximal. We call these
twins of
\epsilon
-type
.

We will present detailed examples for each family of twin algebras in
Section

V
.

III.1
Twins of
H
-Type

The first class of twin algebras results from a choice of non-conjugate subgroups
H
. Our construction relies on so-called Gassmann triples, which allow us to identify both Lagrangian and non-maximal condensable twin algebras. In fact, we show that the subgroups for twin algebras necessarily form Gassmann triples. Examples will be discussed in
Section

V.1
.

III.1.1
General Considerations

Consider a group
G
and two non-conjugate subgroups
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
of
G
.
Consequently by Lemma
II.5
it follows that
A(H_{1},N_{1},\gamma_{1},\epsilon_{1})
and
A(H_{2},N_{2},\gamma_{2},\epsilon_{2})
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
are not isomorphic condensable algebras.
Here we are interested in the question when they have the same anyon decomposition, i.e. the same character (
II.17
).
In general this condition can be satisfied in various ways. However, if
\omega=1
then any Lagrangian algebra with trivial
\gamma
arising from non-conjugate subgroups
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
has to come from a so-called Gassmann triple – which we will discuss in the next section.

We find the following:
if
N=1
then with or without anomaly
\omega
,
H_{i}
have to form a Gassmann triple, in order for the associated algebras be twins.
For non-trivial
N
, then both
N
and
H
have to form Gassmann triples.
We will provide more precise statements in the following, in particular Proposition
III.3
and Proposition
III.4
.

III.1.2
Gassmann Triple Twin Algebras

One approach to construct twin algebras from non-conjugate subgroups uses so-called Gassmann triples.
The non-maximal ones provide examples of twins with possibly inequivalent reduced TOs, see
Section

III.1.3
for examples.

Let us review the basic notions of Gassmann triples first, which appeared in the study of arithmetically equivalent number fields in
Gassmann:1926
. We will adopt the following definition
Sunada1985RiemannianCA
.

Definition III.1
.

Let
G
be a finite group,
H_{1},H_{2}\subset G
two subgroups. The triple
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
is called a
Gassmann triple
if

|H_{1}\cap[g]|=|H_{2}\cap[g]|

(III.1)

for all conjugacy classes
[g]\subset G
. A Gassmann triple
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
is said to be
non-trivial
if
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are not conjugate to each other.

Equivalently,
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
is a Gassmann triple if and only if
\text{Ind}^{G}_{H_{1}}1\cong\text{Ind}^{G}_{H_{2}}1
as
G
-representations.

Remark III.2
.

Note that for a Gassmann triple
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
,
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are of the same order, hence the Hall’s marriage theorem
hall_combinatorial_1998
guarantees a common set
R
of coset representatives for both
G/H_{1}
and
G/H_{2}
to exist.

Gassmann triples are closely related to twin algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
. In fact, twin algebras necessarily carry information of Gassmann triples.

Proposition III.3
.

If two condensable algebras
A(H_{i},N_{i},\gamma_{i},\epsilon_{i})
,
i=1,2
, in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
are twins, then
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
and
(G,N_{1},N_{2})
are necessarily (possibly trivial) Gassmann triples.

Proof.

Twin algebras have the same anyon decompositions, equivalently, they have the same characters. That is,
\chi_{A(H_{1},N_{1},\gamma_{1},\epsilon_{1})}(f,g)=\chi_{A(H_{2},N_{2},\gamma_{2},\epsilon_{2})}(f,g)
for any commuting
f,g\in G
.
For
i=1,2
, fix
R_{i}
a set of representative of
G/H_{i}
.
Evaluate the character (
II.17
) on
(1,g)
for
g\in G
,

\begin{split}\chi_{A(H_{i},N_{i},\gamma_{i},\epsilon_{i})}(1,g)&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in R_{i}\\
{}^{\bar{y}}g\in H_{i}\end{subarray}}1\\
&=\frac{|[g]\cap H_{i}|\times|C_{G}(g)|}{|H_{i}|}\\
&=\chi_{\text{Ind}^{G}_{H_{i}}1}(g).\end{split}

Requiring
\chi_{A(H_{1},N_{1},\gamma_{1},\epsilon_{1})}(1,g)=\chi_{A(H_{2},N_{2},\gamma_{2},\epsilon_{2})}(1,g)
for all
g\in G
is the same as
\text{Ind}^{G}_{H_{1}}1\cong\text{Ind}^{G}_{H_{2}}1
as
G
-representations, hence
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
forms a Gassmann triple.

Similarly, on
(g,1)
,

\begin{split}\chi_{A(H_{i},N_{i},\gamma_{i},\epsilon_{i})}(g,1)&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in R_{i}\\
{}^{\bar{y}}g\in N_{i}\end{subarray}}1\\
&=\frac{|N_{i}|}{|H_{i}|}\chi_{\text{Ind}^{G}_{N_{i}}1}(g).\end{split}

(III.2)

Being twin algebras, two algebras need to be isomorphic as
G
-graded vector spaces. This implies
|N_{1}|=|N_{2}|
and hence
|H_{1}|=|H_{2}|
. Requiring
\chi_{A(H_{1},N_{1},\gamma_{1},\epsilon_{1})}(g,1)=\chi_{A(H_{2},N_{2},\gamma_{2},\epsilon_{2})}(g,1)
for all
g\in G
implies
\text{Ind}^{G}_{N_{1}}1\cong\text{Ind}^{G}_{N_{2}}1
as
G
-representations. We conclude
(G,N_{1},N_{2})
is a Gassmann triple.
∎

The previous result says twin algebras are necessarily labeled by Gassmann triples. For the converse direction, each non-trivial Gassmann triple
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
defines a pair of non-maixmal twin algebras of the form
A(H_{i},1,1,1)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
, whose anyon decompositions are given by the induced representations
5
5

5

Given a representation
\rho
of
H\subseteq G
, one can define the induced representation
\text{Ind}^{G}_{H}\rho={\mathbb{C}}[G]\otimes_{{\mathbb{C}}[H]}\rho
of
G

serre_linear_1977
. In terms of simple
G
-representations
\{\pi_{i}\}_{i}
,
\text{Ind}^{G}_{H}\rho\cong\bigoplus_{i}n_{i}\pi_{i}
with multiplicity
n_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
can be computed by the Frobenius reciprocity
n_{i}=\langle\pi_{i},\text{Ind}^{G}_{H}\rho\rangle_{G}=\langle\text{Res}^{G}_{H}\pi_{i},\rho\rangle_{H}
. Here
\text{Res}^{G}_{H}
denotes the restriction of a
G
-representation to
H
.

\text{Ind}^{G}_{H_{i}}1
,
i=1,2
. The precise meaning will be made explicit in the proof of the following proposition.

Proposition III.4

(Nonmaximal Gassmann-triple twin algebras)
.

Let
G
be a finite group and
\omega\in H^{3}(G,U(1))
.
Two non-maximal condensable algebras
A(H_{1},1,1,1)
and
A(H_{2},1,1,1)

in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
are twin algebras if and only if

(G,H_{1},H_{2})
is a non-trivial Gassmann triple.

Proof.

Let
H\subset G
be any subgroup of
G
, fix a set
R
of representative of coset
G/H
.
For
A(H,1,1,1)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
,
\omega
assumed to be normalized, the
\omega
- and
\epsilon
-phases in its character
\chi_{A(H,1,1,1)}
formula (
II.17
) are trivial.
\chi_{A(H,1,1,1)}
evaluates to the following expression which is closely related to
\chi_{\text{Ind}^{G}_{H}1}
, character of the induced
G
-representation
\text{Ind}^{G}_{H}1
:

\displaystyle\chi_{A(H,1,1,1)}(f,g)

\displaystyle=\delta_{f,1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in R,\\
{}^{\bar{y}}g\in H\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle=\delta_{f,1}\frac{|[g]\cap H|\times|C_{G}(g)|}{|H|}

\displaystyle=\delta_{f,1}\chi_{\text{Ind}^{G}_{H}1}(g),

(III.3)

where
f,g\in G
are such that
fg=gf
. From definition of a Gassmann triple
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
,
\text{Ind}^{G}_{H_{1}}1\cong\text{Ind}^{G}_{H_{2}}1
. Hence, we conclude that
A(H_{1},1,1,1)
and
A(H_{2},1,1,1)
have the same anyon decomposition if and only if
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
is a Gassmann triple. Explicitly,

A(H_{i},1,1,1)\cong\bigoplus_{\rho\in\text{Irrep}(G)}\langle\rho,\text{Ind}^{G}_{H_{i}}1\rangle_{G}([1],\rho),

(III.4)

for
i=1,2
, where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{G}
denotes the inner product of characters over
G
.

A(H_{1},1,1,1)
and
A(H_{2},1,1,1)
with the same character are non-isomorphic if and only if
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
is a non-trivial Gassmann triple.
∎

Gassmann triples provide a rich source of examples of twin algebras. A summary of Gassmann triples
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
with
|G/H_{i}|\leq 15
can be found in
Bosma:2002GassmannNF
.
The smallest-rank example can be found for
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\ltimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{8}})
, which we study in detail in
Section

VI.3
.

If a Gassmann triple
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
has additional properties, they give rise to twin Lagrangian algebras
A(H_{1},1)
and
A(H_{2},1)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
:

Proposition III.5

(Gassmann-triple twin Lagrangians)
.

Two Lagrangian algebras
\mathcal{L}(H_{1},1)
and
\mathcal{L}(H_{2},1)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
are twins if and only if the non-trivial Gassmann triple
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
satisfies that for all abelian subgroup
A
of
G
generated by two elements, the number of
A
-fixed cosets

|(G/H_{1})^{A}|=|(G/H_{2})^{A}|.

(III.5)

Proof.

Character formula (
II.17
) for
\mathcal{L}(H_{i},1)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
simplifies to

\chi_{\mathcal{L}(H_{i},1)}(f,g)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in G/H\\
f^{y},g^{y}\in H\end{subarray}}1

(III.6)

for commuting pair
f,g\in G
,
where the right hand side computes the number of cosets of
H_{i}
fixed simultaneously by
f
and
g
. That is,

\chi_{\mathcal{L}(H_{i},1)}(f,g)=|(G/H_{i})^{\langle f,g\rangle}|\,.

(III.7)

In particular, for
g=1
,
\chi_{\mathcal{L}(H_{i},1)}(f,1)=\Tr(\Ind^G_{H_i}1(f))
. Requiring
\chi_{\mathcal{L}(H_{1},1)}(f,1)=\chi_{\mathcal{L}(H_{2},1)}(f,1)
for all
f\in G
is equivalent to
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
forming a Gassmann triple. Further requiring the characters to agree on any commuting pair
f,g\in G
is equivalent to Gassmann triple
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
satisfying

|(G/H_{1})^{A}|=|(G/H_{2})^{A}|\,,

(III.8)

for all abelian subgroup
A
of
G
generated by two elements.
\mathcal{L}(H_{1},1)
being non-isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}(H_{2},1)
is equivalent to
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
being a non-trivial Gassmann triple.
∎

Remark III.6
.

Condition (
III.5
) can be formulated in terms of (Burnside) marks, see
Pfeiffer01011997
. In this language,
\chi_{A(H,1)}(f,g)=|(G/H)^{\langle f,g\rangle}|
is the number of cosets of
H
fixed simultaneously by
f
and
g
, which is the
mark
of transitive
G
-set
G/H
at
\langle f,g\rangle\subset G
. Condition (
III.5
) requires
G/H_{1}
and
G/H_{2}
to have the same mark at any abelian subgroup of
G
generated by two elements.
Gassmann-triple twin Lagrangian algebras exist, e.g., for group
G=GL(2,3)
or
G
the Clifford group, which we study in detail in the companion paper
WGS
.

Recall from (
II.1
) that non-maximal Gassmann-triple twin algebras
A(H_{1},1,1,1)
and
A(H_{2},1,1,1)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})

are associated to potentially inequivalent reduced TOs
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H_{1}}^{\omega|_{H_{1}}})
and
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H_{2}}^{\omega|_{H_{2}}})
, respectively.

\mathcal{I}_{A(H_{i},1,1,1)}
\mathcal{Z}(G,\omega)
\mathcal{Z}({H_{i}},{\omega|_{H_{i}}})

(III.9)

Such examples with inequivalent reduced TOs do exist, we study them in the next section.

III.1.3
Example with Inequivalent Reduced TOs

Non-trivial Gassmann triples allow us to find examples of twin algebras associated to inequivalent reduced TOs:

\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H_{1}}^{\omega|_{H_{1}}})\not\cong\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H_{2}}^{\omega|_{H_{2}}})\,.

(III.10)

This is achieved using non-trivial Gassmann triples
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
with
H_{1}\not\cong H_{2}
. Hence we conclude that the same collection of anyons can lead to potentially inequivalent
6
6

6

It is possible to have
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H_{1}}^{\omega|_{H_{1}}})\cong\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H_{2}}^{\omega|_{H_{2}}})
for non-isomorphic groups
H_{1}\not\cong H_{2}
, see
Naidu2006CategoricalME
.
reduced TOs.

Example III.7
.

The smallest example of a Gassmann triple
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
with
H_{1}\not\cong H_{2}

(
Sutherland2021Stronger
, Remark 2.9)
has
7
7

7

The GAP ID of these groups are
G:=\text{SmallGroup}(384,5755)
,
H_{1}:=\text{SmallGroup}(16,3)
and
H_{2}:=\text{SmallGroup}(16,10)
.

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=(({\mathbb{Z}}_{4}^{2}\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{4})\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}\,,

(III.11)

\displaystyle H_{1}

\displaystyle=({\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\,,

\displaystyle H_{2}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\,.

The twin algebras
A(H_{1},1,1,1)
and
A(H_{2},1,1,1)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
, lead to inequivalent reduced TOs:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H_{1}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{({\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\rtimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})\,,

(III.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H_{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{{\mathbb{Z}}_{4}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}})\,.

There is furthermore a family of Gassmann triples
(S_{p^{3}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{3},H_{3}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p}))
for any odd prime integer
p
, see
Section

V.1.3
.

These twin algebras are necessarily not related to each other by any braided autoequivalences on
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
. This is because two algebras related by braided autoequivalences results in equivalent reduced TOs
8
8

8

This statement can be checked by concatenation of commutative diagrams.
. This is in contrast to twin algebras studied in the literature constructed from Bogomolov-multiplier autoequivalence
Pollmann:2012
;
Davydov:2013xov
;
Cong:2017ffh
;
Kobayashi:2025ykb
;
Kobayashi:2025pxs
, which we review in
Section

III.3
.

III.2
Twins of
N
-Type

We can consider algebras, non-maximal ones where
H
is the same, but
N_{i}
are non-conjugate normal subgroups in
H
. Examples will be discussed in
Section

V.2
.

As a result of Prop.
III.3
, we know that if two condensable algebras
A(H,N_{i},\gamma_{i},\epsilon_{i})
,
i=1,2
, in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
are twins, then
(G,N_{1},N_{2})
is necessarily a Gassmann triple.

However, not every Gassmann triple
(G,N_{1},N_{2})
results in
A(H,N_{i},\gamma_{i},\epsilon_{i})
being twins for
i=1,2
. For example, consider
H=G
,
\gamma_{i}=1
and
\epsilon_{i}=1
, then
A(G,N_{1},1,1)
and
A(G,N_{2},1,1)
have the same anyon decompositions if and only if
N_{1}=N_{2}
, i.e., they are isomorphic algebras. For subgroups
H\subsetneq G
, it is possible for
(G,N_{1},N_{2})
to be non-trivial Gassmann triples and we will provides examples in
Section

V.2
.

III.3
Twins of
\gamma
-Type

We now turn to algebras that have the same subgroups
H
and
N
, but differ in the cocycle
\gamma
, in the classification data of
A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon)
.
Examples will be discussed in
Section

V.3
.

The anyon decomposition is derived from the
G
-action, equivalently by the character. It depends on
\gamma
in the following way: a 2-cochain
\gamma
on
N
governs the algebra multiplication (
II.7
). The
G
-action is required to be compatible with the multiplication, hence algebras with different
\gamma
generally have different anyon decomposition. However, a
\gamma
with special properties can result in non-isomorphic algebras with the same anyon decomposition.

Twin
Lagrangian
algebras that we discuss here are closely related to ones previously studied for
\mathcal{L}(G,\gamma_{i})\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
, where the
\gamma
’s in the algebra data differ by a Bogomolov multiplier
Pollmann:2012
;
Davydov:2013xov
;
Cong:2017ffh
;
Kobayashi:2025ykb
;
Kobayashi:2025pxs
. We generalize the previously studied twins slightly to
\mathcal{L}(H,\gamma_{i})\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
, allowing
H\neq G
and non-trivial
\omega
. Moreover we found non-maximal
\gamma
-type twin algebras that are different from the Bogomolov-multiplier type ones in
Section

V.3
.

Consider the following special 2-cocycle
FABogomolov_1988
;
Davydov:2013xov
:

Definition III.8
.

Let
H
be a finite group, a (representative of)
\beta\in H^{2}(H,U(1))
satisfying

\beta(h_{1},h_{2})=\beta(h_{2},h_{1})

(III.13)

for any commuting pair
h_{1},h_{2}\in H
is called a
Bogomolov multiplier of
H
.

Proposition III.9

(Bogomolov-multiplier twin Lagrangian algebras)
.

Two Lagrangian algebras
\mathcal{L}(H,\gamma_{1})
and
\mathcal{L}(H,\gamma_{2})
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
such that

\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}\beta

(III.14)

for some non-trivial Bogomolov multiplier
\beta
of
H
are twin algebras.

Proof.

The existence of Lagrangian algebras
A(H,\gamma_{i})
,
i=1,2
, in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
requires
\omega|_{H}
to be trivial, hence we further assume
\omega
is trivialized on
H
. This guarantees that
\beta\neq\Omega_{g}|_{H}
(recall
\Omega_{g}
from equation (
A.11
) and Lemma
II.5
) for any
g
in the normalizer of
H
, hence that the two Lagrangian algebras are not isomorphic, by Lemma
II.5
.

The following computation (using formula (
II.17
)) shows that these algebras have the same character hence are defined on the same anyons:

\displaystyle\chi_{A(H,\gamma_{1}\beta)}(f,g)

\displaystyle=\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in R,\\
{}^{\bar{y}}f,{}^{\bar{y}}g\in H\end{subarray}}\!\!\frac{\omega({}^{\bar{y}}g,y^{-1}|f)}{\omega(y^{-1},g|f)}\frac{\gamma_{1}({}^{\bar{y}}g,{}^{\bar{y}}f)}{\gamma_{1}({}^{\bar{y}}f,{}^{\bar{y}}g)}\frac{\beta({}^{\bar{y}}g,{}^{\bar{y}}f)}{\beta({}^{\bar{y}}f,{}^{\bar{y}}g)}

\displaystyle=\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in R,\\
{}^{\bar{y}}f,{}^{\bar{y}}g\in H\end{subarray}}\!\!\frac{\omega({}^{\bar{y}}g,y^{-1}|f)}{\omega(y^{-1},g|f)}\frac{\gamma_{1}({}^{\bar{y}}g,{}^{\bar{y}}f)}{\gamma_{1}({}^{\bar{y}}f,{}^{\bar{y}}g)}

\displaystyle=\chi_{A(H,\gamma_{1})}(f,g)\,,

(III.15)

for
f,g\in G
such that
fg=gf
.
∎

We comment that Bogomolov multipliers are used in
Davydov:2013xov
to construct non-trivial autoequivalences on
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
that map each object back to itself. This is further used in
Kobayashi:2025pxs
;
Kobayashi:2025ykb
to find twin algebras of the form
\mathcal{L}(G,1)
and
\mathcal{L}(G,\gamma)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
. (Similar structure has been noticed in
Pollmann:2012
.) Such twin algebras are special cases of Proposition
III.9
for
H=G
and
\omega=1
.

The physical implication of the different multiplication structures of such twin algebras with
H=G
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
was studied in
Pollmann:2012
;
Kobayashi:2025pxs
;
Kobayashi:2025ykb
.
The corresponding twin phases
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Vec}_{G}}_{\mathcal{L}(H,\gamma)}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Vec}_{G}}_{\mathcal{L}(H,\gamma\beta)}
can be distinguished from generalized string order parameters
Pollmann:2012
.

III.4
Twins of
\epsilon
-Type

Finally, there are twins that differ only by the
\epsilon
datum.
These are necessarily non-maximal, since for all Lagrangian algebras,
\epsilon
is uniquely fixed by
\gamma
. Examples will be discussed in
Section

V.4
.

We find the following twin algebras that differ in
\epsilon
:

Proposition III.10
.

For non-isomorphic condensable algebras
A(G,N,\gamma,\epsilon_{1})
and
A(G,N,\gamma,\epsilon_{2})
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
, i.e. when
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{2}
do not satisfy (
II.10
), if

\epsilon_{1}(g,n)=\epsilon_{2}(g,n)

(III.16)

for all
n\in N
and
g\in C_{G}(n)
, then they are twins.

Proof.

Equation (
II.13
) implies the multiplicity
n_{([x],\rho)}
of the simple anyon
([x],\rho)
in the algebra
A(G,N,\gamma,\epsilon_{i})
,
i=1,2
, is
0
for
[x]\not\subset N
, and for
[x]\subset N
, the multiplicity
n_{([x],\rho)}
evaluates to
9
9

9

For
H=G
, we fix the set of representative of
G/H
to be
R=\{1\}
.

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in[x],\\
g\in C_{G}(f)\end{subarray}}\biggl\{\frac{\overline{\Tr(\rho_{f}(g))}}{|G|}\epsilon_{i}(g,f)\biggr\}.

(III.17)

Note that because
\epsilon_{1}(g,n)=\epsilon_{2}(g,n)
for any
n\in N
and
g\in C_{G}(n)
, the above number is the same for
i=1,2
. This shows that the two algebras have the same anyon decomposition.

Alternatively, one can observe directly from the character formula (
II.17
) that (
III.16
) guarantees that
A(G,N,\gamma,\epsilon_{1})
and
A(G,N,\gamma,\epsilon_{2})
have the same character, hence the same anyon decomposition.
∎

The smallest rank example for such twins to exist is in TO
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\ltimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{8}})
, the same group
({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\ltimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{8}
that we will discuss in detail in
Section

VI
which is also the smallest group admitting a non-trivial Gassmann triple.

These twins can be detected using generalized string order parameters similar to the ones studied in
Haegeman:2012pex
;
Pollmann:2012
.
To this end, we recall the physical interpretation of the
H
-action phase
\epsilon
studied in
Wen:2023otf
, which measures the charge of the localized edge action. More explicitly, an
N
-symmetry action
U_{n}
splits into edge actions
U_{n}=U_{n}^{L}\otimes U_{n}^{R}
. Introduce notation
\widetilde{U}_{n}:=U_{n}^{L}
. The edge
\widetilde{U}_{n}
of
U_{n}
transforms under
U_{h}
, for
h\in H
, as

U_{h}^{\phantom{-1}}\!\!\!\!\widetilde{U}_{n}^{\phantom{-1}}\!\!\!\!U_{h}^{-1}=\epsilon(h,n)\widetilde{U}_{{}^{h}n}.

(III.18)

Note that if
h
and
n
commute and (
III.16
) holds then,

U_{h}^{\phantom{-1}}\!\!\!\!\widetilde{U}_{n}^{\phantom{-1}}\!\!\!\!U_{h}^{-1}\widetilde{U}_{n}^{-1}=\epsilon_{1}(h,n)=\epsilon_{2}(h,n),

(III.19)

hence if
\epsilon_{i}
’s satisfy equation (
III.16
), the commutator on the left hand side of (
III.19
) does not detect the difference between the above twin algebras.

As commented in
Wen:2023otf
, the
\gamma
and
\epsilon
data can be used as invariants for a gSPT phase, however the charges defined by
\epsilon
on commuting group elements do not determine the phase completely. Our new examples presented in the current section provide gSPTs of this type for a group of order 32.

To distinguish two gSPT twin phases
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Vec}_{G}}_{A_{i}}
,
i=1,2
, where
A_{1}=A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon_{1})
and
A_{2}=A(H,N,\gamma,\epsilon_{2})
are twin algebras satisfying conditions in Proposition
III.10
, one needs generalized string order parameters of the form

U_{h_{1}}^{\phantom{-1}}\!\!\widetilde{U}_{n_{1}}^{\phantom{-1}}\!\!U_{h_{1}}^{-1}\widetilde{U}_{n_{1}}^{-1}\cdots U_{h_{k}}^{\phantom{-1}}\!\!\widetilde{U}_{n_{k}}^{\phantom{-1}}\!\!U_{h_{k}}^{-1}\widetilde{U}_{n_{k}}^{-1}=\prod_{i}^{k}\epsilon(h_{i},n_{i})\text{id}\,.

(III.20)

for
h_{i}\in H
,
n_{i}\in N
,
i\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, such that
h_{1}^{\phantom{-1}}\!\!\!\!n_{1}^{\phantom{-1}}\!\!\!\!h_{1}^{-1}n_{1}^{-1}\cdots h_{k}^{\phantom{-1}}\!\!\!\!n_{k}^{\phantom{-1}}\!\!\!\!h_{k}^{-1}n_{k}^{-1}=1
, guaranteeing that the product (
III.20
) is proportional to the identity operator, with gauge-invariant coefficient, hence can be used to detect the different twin algebras. We illustrate this with examples in
Section

VI.2
.

IV
Twin Phases and Physical Implications

There are several general implications of twin algebras when plugged into the SymTFT paradigm. We will first discuss these in generality, before considering concrete examples.

IV.1
Twin Phases

Definition IV.1

(Twin gapped phases.)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{i}
,
i=1,2
be twin Lagrangian algebras for any SymTFT
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
for a fusion category
\mathcal{S}
. The interval compactification of the SymTFT with
\mathcal{L}^{\text{phys}}=\mathcal{L}_{i}
and a fixed symmetry boundary
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}^{\text{sym}}
give rise to gapped phases
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}^{\mathcal{S}}
with symmetry
\mathcal{S}
, which are called
twin gapped phases
.

The schematic picture is as follows:

(IV.1)

In particular, the inequivalence of the Lagrangians implies that the resulting phases are inequivalent: One way to argue this is that the algebra of order parameters is different. We will discuss this in the next subsection.
An alternative way to phrase this is, that the associated module categories of the symmetry
\mathcal{S}
are inequivalent – this uses the identification of gapped phases with module categories.

We can argue this for group-theoretical symmetries, i.e.
\mathcal{S}
such that
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
. We focus on two non-conjugate subgroups
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
that are preserved in the gapped phase
\mathcal{P}^{\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}}_{\mathcal{L}(H_{i},1)}
,
i=1,2
(this will be the situation for several twin phases and thus instructive to recall).
For each phase, we can take the ground state basis of local operators to be
10
10

10

Here, for each object
a
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
and for
g\in G
, we identify the vector space
\text{Hom}_{\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}}(F_{A(1,1)}(a),{\mathbb{C}}_{g})
with the
g
-graded vector space component of
a
.

\{v_{r,1}\}_{r\in R}
, following notation from (
II.3
), where
R
is a common set of representatives of left cosets
G/H_{i}
for
i=1,2
(whose existence is guaranteed, see Remark
III.2
).
From the multiplication structure (
II.7
) the
v_{r,1}
are idempotents

v_{r,1}v_{r^{\prime},1}=\delta_{rH_{i},r^{\prime}H_{i}}\;v_{r,1}\,,

(IV.2)

and thus physically correspond to projectors onto orthogonal vacua (also called universes in the gapless setting).
On these idempotents, it is explicit to check that subgroups conjugate to
H_{i}
act trivially on these bases: this follows from the
G
-action (
II.6
) and relation (
II.2
)

\displaystyle(rh_{i}r^{-1})\cdot v^{i}_{r,1}=v^{i}_{rh_{i},1}=v^{i}_{r,1}\,,

(IV.3)

for all
h_{i}\in H_{i}
. This shows that in the vacuum defined by
v^{i}_{r,1}
the symmetry
rH_{i}r^{-1}
is preserved. Conversely, for
k_{i}\notin H_{i}
, one has

\displaystyle(rk_{i}r^{-1})\cdot v^{i}_{r,1}=v^{i}_{rk_{i},1}\neq v^{i}_{r,1}\,,

(IV.4)

since
rk_{i}\notin rH_{i}
. This shows that group elements that are not in
rH_{i}r^{-1}
act non-trivially on
v_{r,1}^{i}
: the symmetries they generate are therefore spontaneously broken therein. In particular the phases associated to Lagrangian algebras
\mathcal{L}(H_{1},1)
and
\mathcal{L}(H_{2},1)
with
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
non-conjugate in
G
are not related by a continuous path of symmetric Hamiltonians without closing the gap, i.e. are inequivalent gapped phases. We can argue similarly for projective representations, i.e. in the presence of
\gamma
. For more general symmetries that may not be group-theoretical, one can argue that the algebra of order parameters is different, which we will discuss in the next subsection.

Gapless Twin Phases.

We can furthermore study interfaces and associated gapless phases, that are twins. We will discuss these in the examples more concretely.
In particular we find that
\epsilon
-type twin algebras, which necessarily are non-Lagrangian algebras, give rise to twin gapless SPT (gSPT) phases. In order to distinguish such gapless phases, we need to determine a generalized string order parameter, which does not rely on the anyons, but is detected from the action of the symmetry on the order parameters.

IV.2
The Algebra of Order Parameters

One way to distinguish twin phases is to consider the operator product algebra of order parameters (OPs), see e.g.,
Chavda:2026mpz
. In fact the definition of twins as algebras that are isomorphic as objects, i.e. arising from the same generalized charges, but being not isomorphic as algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
(multiplicative structure), indicates this already.
Here we will first discuss the algebra structure on condensable algebras in some more detail before applying these to twins. It turns out that in order to study the algebra of OPs, it is in fact entirely unnecessary to consider anyons.

Algebra structure of condensable algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
is determined by viewing them as
G
-graded vector spaces (
II.7
). This translates to multiplication on the vector space of local operators in a
\mathcal{S}
-symmetric phase
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S}}_{A}
, for any group-theoretical fusion category
\mathcal{S}
.

The space of local operators in
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}}
can be recovered from the algebras
\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}
in the following way. The symmetry Lagrangian algebra for
\mathcal{S}
, denoted
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}=\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}
, in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
singles out a monoidal forgetful functor
11
11

11

This can be obtained from the free module functor
-\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}

davydov2013witt
.

F_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}}:\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})\rightarrow\mathcal{S},

(IV.5)

which projects the anyon lines in the TO to the symmetry boundary
\mathfrak{B}^{\text{sym}}
. For each symmetry defect
X
in
\mathcal{S}
, the space of
X
-twisted local operators in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}}^{\mathcal{S}}
is

(V^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}})_{X}:=\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigl(F_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}),X\bigr).

(IV.6)

For a simple anyon
a
in
\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}
, the
\mathcal{S}
symmetry action on an
X
-twisted operator
\mathcal{O}\in\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{S}}(F_{A_{\mathcal{S}}}(a),X)\subset(V^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}})_{X}
depends only on
12
12

12

See, e.g.,
Bhardwaj:2023idu
;

. This statement is further made explicit in terms of representations of tube categories in
Bartsch:2026wqq
.

a
.
Hence the space of
X

\mathcal{S}

13

Equivalently, decompose with respect to generalized charge/tube representation
Bartsch:2022mpm
;

.
into

(V^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}})_{X}\cong\bigoplus_{a\in\text{Irr}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S}))}(V^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}})_{a,X}

(IV.7)

where
(V^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}})_{a,X}:=n_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}},a}\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{S}}(F_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}}(a),X)
.
In particular, the space of local operators (i.e., local operators in untwisted sector) in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}}^{\mathcal{S}}
is

\begin{split}(V^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}})_{1}&=\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{S}}\bigl(F_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{S}}}(\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}),1\bigr)\\
&\cong\bigoplus_{a\in\text{Irr}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S}))}(V^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}})_{a,1}\,.\end{split}

(IV.8)

Multiplication on the space of local operators can be recovered from the coalgebra structure on
\mathcal{L}_{\text{phys}}=A
. Let
F^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}}
denote the monoidal structure of the forgetful functor
F_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}}
. For
X,Y
in
\mathcal{C}
, two local operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{1}

\displaystyle\in\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(F_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}}(A),X),

(IV.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{2}

\displaystyle\in\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(F_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}}(A),Y),

(IV.10)

in the
X
- and
Y
-twisted sectors, respectively,
can be multiplied to a local operator

(\mathcal{O}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{2})\circ(F^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}})_{A,A}^{-1}\circ F(\Delta),

(IV.11)

in the
X\otimes Y
-twisted sector
\text{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(F_{\mathcal{L}_{\text{sym}}}(A),X\otimes Y)
, where
\Delta:A\rightarrow A\otimes A
is the comultiplication.

For
\mathcal{S}=\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega}
, the symmetry algebra is
\mathcal{L}(1,1)
and the forgetful functor

, and the multiplication on the vector space of

).

IV.3
No Relative SSB and non-Landau Transitions

One crucial implication of twin gapped phases is that irrespective of the symmetry boundary the symmetry breaking patterns for the phases
\mathcal{P}_{i}
are the same. In particular, twin phases cannot be distinguished by relative spontaneous symmetry breaking (SSBing).
Here we define
no relative SSBing
as the property of two gapped phases, which for any choice of gauge related (Morita equivalent) symmetry category, have the same symmetry breaking pattern. In particular they both have the same number of vacua.

Naturally, phases with no relative SSBing are perfect candidates for
phase transitions with no hidden symmetry breaking
14
14

14

Hidden symmetry breaking occurs when two topological
\mathcal{C}
-symmetric phases are indistinguishable by local operators, but their gauged phases are distinguishable by symmetry breaking patterns. E.g., this is the case between the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-symmetric trivial and non-trivial SPT phases
Kennedy:1992ifl
.
, although in order for there to be a second order transition, we require some additional structure (e.g. an anomaly). This is discussed in the companion paper
WGS
.

Proposition IV.2

(No relative SSBing)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
be Lagrangian algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
.
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}
have no relative SSBing
if and only if
\mathcal{L}_{1}\cong\mathcal{L}_{2}
as objects in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
, i.e. they are twin Lagrangian algebras.

In particular, for any symmetry
\mathcal{S}
that is Morita equivalent to
\mathcal{C}
the phases
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}
have the same symmetry breaking pattern.

Proof.

Assuming
\mathcal{L}_{1}\not\cong\mathcal{L}_{2}
as objects in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
, then there exists
a\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
such that

n_{\mathcal{L}_{1},a}\neq n_{\mathcal{L}_{2},a}.

(IV.12)

Let
\mathcal{C}_{1}
denote the fusion category corresponding to
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, then the two subspaces

(V_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}^{\mathcal{C}_{1}})_{a,1}\not\cong(V_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{\mathcal{C}_{1}})_{a,1}

(IV.13)

are inequivalent, showing that
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{C}_{1}}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{C}_{1}}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}
admit distinct symmetry breaking patterns. We conclude either
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}
have distinct symmetry breaking pattern, or if they have the same symmetry breaking pattern, they can be gauged to
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{C}_{1}}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{C}_{1}}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}
with different symmetry breaking pattern, hence admitting relative SSBing.
For any
\mathcal{S}
symmetry dual to
\mathcal{C}
and
a\in\text{Irr}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}))
,
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}
have isomorphic spaces
(V_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}^{{\mathcal{S}}})_{a,1}\cong(V_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{{\mathcal{S}}})_{a,1}
of local operators, with respect to the
\mathcal{S}
symmetry. Therefore there is no relative SSBing between
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{C}_{1}}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{C}_{1}}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}
as they admit the same symmetry breaking pattern for all symmetry categories related to
\mathcal{C}
by gauging.
∎

There is a bijection between Lagrangian algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
and indecomposable
\mathcal{C}
-module categories
davydov2013witt
. Denote by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{C}}
the
\mathcal{C}
-module category corresponding to Lagrangian algebra
\mathcal{L}
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a fusion category. The topological
\mathcal{C}
-symmetric phases
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{L}}
are in bijection to
\mathcal{C}
-module categories
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{C}}

Thorngren:2019iar
. Phase
\mathcal{P}^{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathcal{L}}
has vector space of vacua of dimension
\rank\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{C}}
.
Hence the twin topological phases defined above have the following properties:

If
\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
are twin Lagrangian algebras in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
, then

\rank\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}^{\mathcal{S}}=\rank\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{\mathcal{S}}

(IV.14)

for any fusion category
\mathcal{S}
Morita dual
15
15

15

See Appendix
B.1.2
for the definition of Morita duals.
to
\mathcal{C}
.

V
Examples of Twin Algebras

We now give examples of twin algebras of all the different types we studied in general in section
III
. We focus on twins for groups of order
<64
as those will not fall into the category of known twins arising from Bogomolnov multipliers. We will consider group with and without anomalies. The search was done using GAP.
A complete list of twins in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
for
|G|\leq 48
is provided in
Section

V.5
.

V.1
Examples of
H
-type Twins

As we explained these
H
-type twins arise from non-conjugate subgroups. For groups without anomalies, by Proposition
III.4
, they come from Gassmann triples.

V.1.1
G=({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\ltimes{\mathbb{Z}}_{8}
with and without
\omega

for which twin algebras occur in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G}^{\omega})
is
32
.

V.1.2
G=GL(2,3)
with and without
\omega

This group is the main focus of the companion paper

WGS
. It is the general linear group on two by two matrices

G=GL(2,3)=Q_{8}\rtimes S_{3}

(V.2)

over the field
\mathbb{F}_{3}
. The key property is that it has two non-conjugate subgroups, that are isomophic as groups (permutation groups), but non-conjugate in
G

S_{3}^{(1)}\not\sim S_{3}^{(2)}\,.

(V.3)

We find that there are Gassmann triple algebras for both
G
and
G
with anomaly. The details are provided in Tab.
1
for
G
without anomaly. With anomaly, the condensable algebras are fewer, and the twins are:

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\text{Twin 1}&\text{Twin 2}&\text{Reduced TO}\cr\hline\cr\hline\cr A(S_{3}^{(1)},1,1,1)&A(S_{3}^{(2)},1,1,1)&D({S_{3}})\cr\hline\cr A(S_{3}^{(1)},{\mathbb{Z}}_{3},1,1)&A(S_{3}^{(2)},{\mathbb{Z}}_{3},1,1)&D({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})\cr\hline\cr A(S_{3}^{(1)},S_{3}^{(1)},1,1)&A(S_{3}^{(2)},S_{3}^{(2)},1,1)&\text{Trivial}\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

(V.4)

We study the physical implications of the twin gapped phases in
WGS
, where in the presence of a mixed-anomaly, we note that the transition between the twin gapped phases is beyond-Landau without hidden symmetry breaking.

V.1.3
G=S_{p^{3}}

There is a family of non-trivial Gassmann triples
(G,H_{1},H_{2})
, indexed by an odd prime, with
H_{1}
abelian and
H_{2}
non-abelian
Komatsu:1976adelering
. For each odd prime number
p
,
G=S_{p^{3}}
denotes the symmetric group. Consider

H_{1}\cong({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptH_{2}\cong\bm{H}_{3}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})\,,

(V.5)

where
\bm{H}_{3}
is the Heisenberg group, i.e., the group of
3\times 3
upper triangular matrices over
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, with diagonal entries being 1. Regarding both groups as subgroups of
S_{p^{3}}
, where both groups act on themselves as a set of cardinality
p^{3}
, via left multiplication,
(S_{p^{3}},({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3},\bm{H}_{3}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p}))
is a non-trivial Gassmann triple. Hence the condensable algebras
A(({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3},1,1,1)
and
A(\bm{H}_{3}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p}),1,1,1)
in
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{S_{p^{3}}})
are twins, but their condensations result in inequivalent reduced TOs, as discussed in
Section

III.1.3
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H_{1}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})^{3}})

(V.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{H_{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{\bm{H}_{3}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})})\,.

V.2
Examples of
N
-type Twins

The group
GL(2,3)
with trivial anomaly
\omega=1
also provides examples of
N
-type twins:

A(D_{12},S_{3}^{(1)},1,1)\,,\hskip 20.00003ptA(D_{12},S_{3}^{(2)},1,1)

(V.7)

are twins that have the same
H
, but non-conjugate
N
.

They have the anyon decomposition

([1],1)\oplus([1],\rho_{3})\oplus([h],1_{++})\oplus([a],1_{++})

(V.8)

Another group admitting twins of
N
-type is

G_{48,49}={\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{2}\times A_{4}=\langle f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4},f_{5}\rangle

(V.9)

for the algebras

\displaystyle A({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{4}=\langle f_{1},f_{2},f_{4},f_{5}\rangle,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{2}=\langle f_{1}f_{4},f_{2}f_{5}\rangle,1,1)\,,

(V.10)

\displaystyle A({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{4}=\langle f_{1},f_{2},f_{4},f_{5}\rangle,{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{2}=\langle f_{4}f_{1}f_{5},f_{2}f_{5}\rangle,1,1)

with anyon decomposition

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\cong([1],1)\oplus([1],1f_{3}=e^{4\pi i/3})\oplus([1],1f_{3}=e^{2\pi i/3})

(V.11)

\displaystyle\oplus([f_{1}f_{4}],1)\oplus([f_{2}f_{4}],1)\oplus([f_{1}f_{2}f_{4}],1)\,.

V.3

\gamma
-type Twins

The algebras that differ by the SPT
\gamma
and
H=G
inside
\mathcal{Z}(\mathsf{Vec}_{G})
were studied previously in
Pollmann:2012
;
Kobayashi:2025pxs
;
